6         EMPLOYEE RESPONSIBILITIES



6.1       ARREST, INDICTMENT, CONVICTION



6.2       ATTENDANCE AND ABSENTEEISM

PURPOSE

Delta County relies on the dependable attendance of its employees to maintain high-quality public services, maintain
operational efficiency, and ensure adequate staffing levels across all county facilities. The purpose of this policy is to
establish clear standards for attendance, define proper notification procedures for absences, and outline the corrective
action framework for unexcused absenteeism and no-call/no show incidents.

APPLICABILITY

This policy applies to all regular full-time, regular part-time, seasonal, temporary, and per diem employees of Delta
County.

      •   Employees of Elected Officials: Staff under the direction of Elected Officials are subject to this policy unless the
          respective Elected Official has issued a separate written office policy governing attendance.
      •   Collective Bargaining Agreements: For union-represented personnel, attendance, tardiness, and absence
          notification rules established in the operative agreement take precedence where direct conflicts exist.

REPORTING PROCEDURES AND EXPECTATIONS

Standard Absence Notifications:

Employees are expected to work their scheduled hours consistently. When an emergency, illness, or unavoidable
circumstance prevents an employee from reporting to work on time or for their entire shift:

      •   Timeframe: the employee must notify their immediate supervisor as far in advance as possible, but no later than
          thirty (30) minutes prior to their scheduled shift.
      •   Method: notification must be made via telephone call or text communication with the supervisor.
      •   Content: the employee must specify the date/shift of the absence and the anticipated date/time of return.

Excused vs. Unexcused Absences:

      •   Excused Absences – absences covered by pre-approved benefit time off requests, medical appointments,
          approved Family and Medical Leave (FMLA), statutory Michigan Earned Sick Time, military leave, jury duty, or
          bereavement leave.
      •   Unexcused Absences – any absence that has not ben approved in advance, where the employee lacks sufficient
          accrued leave balances, or where the employee fails to provide required statutory documentation.

NO-CALL/NO-SHOW




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                          Page 25 of 55


A no-call/no-show occurs when an employee fails to report for their scheduled work shift and fails to properly notify
their supervisor in accordance with REPORTING PROCEDURES AND EXPECTATIONS (above) of this policy.

Because unscheduled, unannounced absences severely compromise County operations and force remaining staff to cover
unexpected workloads, No-Call/No-Show occurrences trigger accelerated disciplinary measures within a rolling 12-month
period.

      •   First Occurrence – the employee will receive a formal verbal warning and will be placed on notice regarding job
          abandonment risks. The shift hours missed will be recorded as unpaid leave, unless the employee has benefit
          time to cover the absence.
      •   Second Occurrence – the employee will receive a final written warning, and could receive a 1-to-3-day unpaid
          disciplinary suspension, per the direct supervisor’s discretion.
      •   Third Occurrence – the employee will be subject to immediate termination of employment for job abandonment
          and failure to fulfill employment obligations.

Any employee who fails to report to work and fails to contact their supervisor or the Administration Department for three
(3) consecutive scheduled workdays will be deemed to have voluntarily resigned their employment with Delta County
due to job abandonment. On the third consecutive day of unexcused, unannounced absence, Administration will process
the separation as a voluntary resignation not in good standing, effective as of the end of the last shift worked.

EXCEPTIONS AND EXTENUATING CIRCUMSTANCES

Delta County recognizes that severe, unforeseen emergencies can prevent an employee from contacting their supervisor.
In such a rare instance, the employee (or a family member acting on their behalf), must provide verifiable documentation
showing that it was physically or practically impossible to communicate prior to or during the absence window. Upon
review by the Department Head or Elected Official in conjunction with the Administration Department, disciplinary
actions or job abandonment designations may be mitigated or waived.

9.1       GENERAL BUDGET

PURPOSE

The purpose of this policy is to establish a uniform system for the preparation, adoption, and execution of the County’s
annual budget. This policy ensures fiscal sustainability, transparency, and strict adherence to MCL 141.421 et seq. (the
Uniform Budgeting and Accounting Act).

FISCAL YEAR AND ACCOUNTING BASIS

Fiscal Year: The County’s fiscal year shall begin October 1 and end September 30.

Basis of Accounting: The budget shall be prepared on a modified accrual basis for all governmental funds, consistent
with Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP). Revenues are recognized, when they become measurable and
available, expenditures are recognized when the liability is incurred.

THE BALANCED BUDGET MANDATE

In accordance with Michigan Law (PA 621 of 1978), the County must adopt a balanced budget for the General Fund and
all Special Revenue Funds.

      •   Definition: Total estimated expenditures, including an allowance for emergency contingencies, shall not
          exceed the total estimated revenues plus any available unassigned fund balance from prior years.
      •   Anti-Deficit Rule: The County is prohibited from adopting a budget that creates a deficit in any fund.

BUDGET DEVELOPMENT PROCESS


As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                      Page 28 of 55


The County Administrator (designated as the Chief Administrative Officer under PA 2) is responsible for the budget’s
formulation.

          1.      Budget Initiation: Department Heads shall submit requests to the Administrator by May.
          2.      Administrator’s Review: The Administrator shall review all requests, revenue projections, and debt
                  service requirements to create a Recommended Budget.
          3.      Public Inspection: The proposed budget must be available for public inspection at the County Clerk’s
                  office at least six days prior to the public hearing.
          4.      Public Hearing: Per MCL 141.412, the Board of Commissioners shall hold at least one public hearing.
                  Notice of this hearing must be published in a newspaper of general circulation.
          5.      Adoption: The Board shall adopt the General Appropriations Act no later than the final regularly
                  scheduled meeting in September.

BUDGETARY CONTROL AND AMENDMENTS

      •   Expenditure Limits: No money shall be disbursed except pursuant to the General Appropriations Act. No officer
          or employee shall cause the County to exceed the total appropriation for any department or fund.
      •   Budget Monitoring: The Administrator shall provide the Board with quarterly Budget-to-Actual reports to
          monitor performance.
      •   Amendments: If it is determined that revenues are less than anticipated or expenditures will exceed the
          appropriation, a Budget Amendment Request may be made by a Department Head to the Controller on the
          approved Budget Amendment Request form. Refer to policy 9.7 Journal Entry/Budget Amendments.

CAPITAL IMPROVEMENT PLANNING

The County shall maintain a five-year Capital Improvement Plan, updated annually in conjunction with the annual
budget. Capital projects (items with a life expectancy of 5+ years and a cost exceeding a Board-defined threshold)
shall be prioritized based on safety, legal mandates, and operational efficiency.

INTERNAL CONTROLS

      •   Allotments: The Administrator may use an allotment system to restrict spending to specific periods (e.g.
          quarterly) if fiscal conditions warrant. The allotment system ensures that each department receives its fair
          share of resources based on labor costs, expenditures, revenue, or other measurable factors.
      •   Grant Management: All grant-funded positions or programs must include a “sunset clause” stating that the
          program will terminate if grant funding is withdrawn.

9.2       PURCHASING AND PROCUREMENT

PURPOSE AND SCOPE
To obtain necessary goods and services at the least cost for the effective and efficient operation of the County.

The purchasing policies and procedures shall apply to all County funds under the authority of the Delta County Board of
Commissioners.

RESPONSIBILITY/AUTHORIZATION

The County Controller will serve as the Chief Financial Officer for Delta County. The Chief Financial Officer, or their
designee, is authorized to spend up to and including $10,000 for any departments without obtaining prior approval.

The Chief Financial Officer is authorized to dispose of any County asset less than $1,000 in value by soliciting bids for
purchase of the asset.




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                       Page 29 of 55


It is the responsibility of the Department Heads, Elected Officials, and the Chief Financial Officer to ensure that there
are sufficient funds available in the appropriate activity to procure the goods and services.

In the absence of the Chief Financial Officer, one of the following in this order shall serve as the Chief Financial Officer:
    1.        County Administrator
    2.        County Treasurer
    3.        Finance Committee Chair

DEPARTMENT HEADS AND ELECTED OFFICIALS

Department Heads and Elected Officials are authorized to spend up to and including $3,000 within their department's
budgetary constraints.

A Department Head or Elected Official may not authorize any purchase if they do not have the funds for the purchase
available in their budget. A Department Head or Elected Official is prohibited from making any purchase that would
result in a budgeted line-item to be exceeded without prior written approval of the County Controller and a submission
of a budget amendment request.

Department Heads are as follows: Building and Zoning Director, Veterans Services Director, Emergency Management
Coordinator, Dispatch Director; Friend of the Court, Airport Manager, Maintenance Director, Parks Manager, IT Director,
and Administrator.

Elected Officials are as follows: Clerk/Register of Deeds, Treasurer, Prosecuting Attorney, Sheriff, and Judges.

ADMINISTRATOR

The Delta County Administrator shall process all payment requests. The County Clerk then prepares a bills payable to
the County Board for approval.

TREASURER

The County Treasurer is responsible for co-signing checks along with the County Clerk.

PURCHASING GUIDELINES

         PROCUREMENT PROCESS

         Procurement of goods and services over $1,000 shall be through obtaining quotations from one or more suppliers,
         if available.

         Procurement of goods and services equaling $1,000 and up to $9,999 shall be obtained as follows:

         1.      The Department Head or Elected Official shall submit quote(s) to the County Administrator prior to
                 making the purchase.
         2.      The County Administrator may issue a purchase order authorizing the purchase, declining the request to
                 purchase, request additional quotes, or forward to the Finance Committee for consideration for full
                 Board of Commissioner approval.

         Procurement of goods and services equaling $10,000 but less than $25,000 shall require approval of the County
         Board.

         No purchase shall be authorized or completed prior to receiving the required approval as determined by this
         policy. Any purchase made without the proper approval is a direct violation of this policy.




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                         Page 30 of 55


        Procurement of goods and services equaling $25,000 or greater shall use a competitive bid process in accordance
        with specification listed in an advertisement to bid. A project may not be subdivided into individual items for
        the purpose of circumventing the bid procedure. When appropriate, bid specifications shall be prepared by
        professionals and shall be reviewed by the County Administrator and/or legal counsel prior to being advertised.
        All bids for which an outside professional prepared the bid specifications shall be reviewed by that professional
        and they shall make a recommendation to the Board regarding the bid award.

        All conflicts of interest between Commissioners and Vendors must be disclosed before a Board of Commissioners
        vote for acceptance of a bid proposal.

        The County Board may forgo the competitive bidding process for any of the following reasons:

        1.       State Contract/Government Pricing – the good or service is purchased from a contract that was
                 competitively bid by the State of Michigan or by another government entity.
        2.       Professional/Specialized Services – the procurement of professional services, including but not limited
                 to, legal counsel, engineers, and consultants.
        3.       Sole Source Provider – there is only one provider of the good or service.
        4.       Surplus Property – the equipment being purchased from another governmental, public, or quasi-public
                 entity.
        5.       Grants and Donations – the procurement of goods or services that are 100% from grant or donated
                 funds.
        6.       Preferred Specializations/Limited Providers – the County is seeking a specific make, brand, feature,
                 product, or service that can only be provided by a limited number of suppliers within a reasonable
                 distance.
        7.       Other – situations where the solicitation of bids or proposals would for any reason be impractical,
                 unavailing, or impossible.

        If no bids or proposals are received, the County Board may proceed to have the services performed or the good
        procured without further competitive bidding.

        In all cases, due diligence shall be made in all procurement efforts to obtain the best product or service at the
        best cost for Delta County.

PURCHASE ORDERS

The following pertains to goods and services outside the realm of normal weekly/monthly business transactions.

Procurement of goods and services over $1,000 shall be prepared on a Purchase Order and approved by the Chief Financial
Officer. Procurement of goods and services over $10,000 shall be prepared on Purchase Order and approved by the Delta
County Board of Commissioners.

Purchase orders are to be entered into the financial accounting software and follow the approval process for that
department.

BID/QUOTATION

Awarding any bid or quotation will be based on the following criteria:
       Price
       Specification Requirement
       Product Quality
       Service
       Reputation of Firm or Product




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                       Page 31 of 55


Any other relevant factors the Board of Commissioners may wish to consider.

The Delta County Board of Commissioners reserve the right to reject any and all bids or quotes.

EMERGENCY PURCHASE
In the case of an emergency that must be resolved immediately, as it will endanger the health, safety or welfare of the
people of the County; the Chief Financial Officer shall be empowered to approve such emergency purchases with the
concurrence of the Board Chair and the Finance Committee Chair. Emergency purchases shall be ratified at the next
County Board meeting.

SERVICE CONTRACTS
It shall be the policy of the Board to enter into service agreements with the vendors of the various products the Board
purchases upon the recommendation of the Finance Committee and/or County Administrator.

PAYMENTS
All payment requests shall be forwarded to the County Clerk’s office within five (5) business days of receipt by
Department Heads, Elected Officials, and Chief Financial Officer. The Board Secretary will prepare a bills payable report
to the County Administrator for approval. The County Administrator submits the bills payable report to the County Board
for approval, during a County Board of Commissioners meeting.
Once the Board of Commissioners have approved payment, the County Clerk and Treasurer have five (5) business days to
mail payment to vendor(s).
Certain types of purchases may be approved by the Board of Commissioners with a majority Board vote. Below is a list
of these types of purchases:
        Payroll
        Payroll Remittances
        Insurance Coverage
        Utilities
        All Court Related Fees
        All Trust and Agency Account Payments
        Sheriff’s Department Food Purchases
        Petty Cash Reimbursements
        Mental Health Vouchers
        Department of Social Services Vouchers
        Purchases Approved by the Chief Financial Officer
        Appropriation Payouts (following policy 9.18   Third-Party Appropriations)
        Operating Transfers
        Approved Contracts
These expenditures will be reviewed and approved by the Chief Financial Officer with every budget year.

PETTY CASH FUND

Each department that is approved by the Board of Commissioners for a Petty Cash Fund shall have a limit not to exceed
$200.00. The Department Head/Elected Official shall be responsible for the management and control of the petty cash
fund within their respective department.

CONFLICTS OF INTERESTS

Any employees, officials, officers, and agents of Delta County who are involved in the procurement and selection of bids
and purchases shall avoid real, apparent, or potential conflicts of interest. No employee, official, officer, or agent of
Delta County shall participate in selection, award, or administration of a contract if a conflict of interest, real or



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                      Page 32 of 55


apparent, would be involved. Such a conflict would arise when the employee, official, officer, or agent; any member of
their immediate family; their partner; an organization which employs, or is about to employ, any of the above; has a
financial or personal interest.

An employee, official, officer, or agent of Delta County who is involved in the procurement and selection of a bid or
purchase, and who has a real or apparent conflict of interest must disclose that conflict of interest within the context
of the appropriate committee or board meeting that occurs before the bid selection or purchase takes place. Such
disclosure must be documented in the minutes for that meeting which shall be retained as part of the official record
surrounding the bid or purchase.

Employees, officials, officers, and agents of Delta County will not solicit nor accept gratuities, favors, or anything of
monetary value from contractors, potential contractors, parties of any sub-agreement, or parties of an awarded bid or
potential award for a bid.

Employees, officials, officers, and agents of Delta County are required to follow Michigan law as it pertains to contracts
of public servants with public entities. Reference MCL 15.321 – MCL 15.323.

In addition to this policy, the Delta County Board of Commissioners has also adopted an additional policy pertaining to
County Commissioners and conflicts of interests involving contract and business transactions.

Any employee, official, officer, or agent of Delta County who fails to abide by the following policy may be sanctioned or
disciplined, to the extent permitted by law, for violations of the above standards.

VENDOR PROTESTS

The term vendor protest shall mean an allegation where there has been a breach, misinterpretation, or improper
application of these purchasing policies/guidelines. Prompt and just settlement of the protest is in the mutual interest
of the County and Vendor. Therefore, a procedure has been developed to consider these protests.

      1.     The vendor shall present the complaint orally or written to the County Administrator for resolution. If an oral
             complaint is received, the County Administrator will create a written record of the complaint.
      2.     If the protest is not settled in step 1, the protest shall be reduced to writing and presented to the County
             Administrator and Chair of the County Board of Commissioners within ten (10) business days after an answer
             to step 1 has been given. The written protest shall include the follow:
                       i. Name, address and telephone number of protestor
                      ii. Signature of protestor or its representative
                     iii. Identification of purchasing policy/guideline that was breached
                     iv. Form of relief being sought
      3.     If the protest is not settled in step 2, the vendor may file a written protest to the Finance Committee within
             ten (10) business days of receipt of the County Administrator’s response. The Finance Committee will take
             up the protest at their next regularly scheduled meeting. The Finance Committee will recommend to the
             County Board of Commissioners whether the protest should be upheld or denied. The decision of the County
             Board of Commissioners is final.

9.3        FUND BALANCE USEAGE

PURPOSE

The purpose of this policy is to establish a framework for the management of fund balances within the governmental
funds of Delta County. This policy ensures the County maintains a stable financial base, provides for unanticipated
expenditures or revenue shortfalls, and complies with Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) Statement No.
54.




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                        Page 33 of 55


FUND BALANCE CLASSIFICATIONS

In accordance with GASB 54, the County shall report fund balances in the following five (5) categories:

      •   Nonspendable: Amounts that cannot be spent because they are either (a) not in spendable form or (b) legally
          or contractually required to be maintained intact.
      •   Restricted: Amounts constrained to specific purposes by their providers (such as grantors, bondholders, and
          higher levels of government) through constitutional provisions or enabling legislation.
      •   Committed: Amounts that can only be used for specific purposes pursuant to a formal action (Resolution by the
          Delta County Board of Commissioners, the County’s highest level of decision-making authority).
      •   Assigned: Amounts the County intends to use for a specific purpose but are neither restricted nor committed.
          The Board of Commissioners hereby delegates the authority to assign fund balances to the Chief Financial
          Officer.
      •   Unassigned: The residual classification for the General Fund, representing amounts that are available for any
          purpose.

Target Fund Balance Levels

To maintain fiscal health and protect against economic downturns or emergencies, the County establishes the following
targets for the General Fund:

      1. Minimum Unassigned Fund Balance: The County shall strive to maintain a minimum unassigned fund balance of
         20% of the annual General Fund operating expenditures.
      2. Replenishment: If the unassigned fund balance falls below the 20% threshold, the County shall develop a plan
         within the fiscal year to replenish the balance through budgetary control or revenue enhancement over the
         next two fiscal years.
      3. Excess Funds: If the unassigned fund balance exceeds 20%, the Board of Commissioners may consider one-time
         allocations for capital projects, debt reduction, or emergency reserves.

Order of Expenditures

When an expenditure is incurred for purposes both restricted and unrestricted (committed, assigned, or unassigned)
fund balances are available, it is the policy of Delta County to use restricted resources first, then unrestricted
resources as they are needed.

Within the unrestricted fund balance, the order of use shall be:

      1. Committed
      2. Assigned
      3. Unassigned

Annual Review

The County Administrator shall report the status of the fund balances to the Board of Commissioners during the annual
budget presentation. This policy shall be reviewed annually during every budget process to ensure alignment with
Michigan Department of Treasury guidelines and the County’s long-term financial goals.

9.4       ELECTRONIC BANKING POLICY

PURPOSE




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                     Page 34 of 55


In accordance with Public Act 738 of 2002, Delta County adopts the following policy to ensure and promote safety in
Delta County’s funds in the electronic funds transfer environment, and to update the framework for internal controls
surrounding electronic transactions.

DEFINITIONS

Automatic Clearing House (ACH) and Electronic Funds Transfers (EFT) means the exchange or transfers of money
electronically from one account to another, either within the same organization or across different companies.

POLICY

It is the policy of Delta County to allow for the electronic transfer of funds for the purpose of direct deposit,
withdrawal, transfer, and payment. Payments may be paid via electronic funds transfer if deemed in the best interest
of the County, for example to avoid late fees or to streamline processes.

ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURES

All disbursements of funds using electronic transfer shall be conducted by either the County Treasurer’s office, or the
Administrator or designee in charge of payroll and other electronic transfer transactions. All disbursements require
supporting documentation detailing the goods or services purchased, the cost of the goods or services, the date of the
transfer, the departments and activities serviced by the transfer, and the department head authorizing or contract
authorizing the transfer. Electronic backup documents attached to general ledger entry is considered adequate for the
purposes of this supporting documentation.

All electronic transfers shall be promptly recorded in the general ledger. The County Treasurer shall review all
supporting documentation and reconcile all electronic banking activity to the general ledger.

All ACH invoices shall be approved via the normal Delta County payment procedures with the following exceptions:
Routine payroll, federal and state withholdings, investments, transfer between County accounts, MERS, insurance,
payments made via established and approved contract(s).

9.5       INVESTMENT POLICY

PURPOSE

It is the policy of Delta County to invest public funds in a manner which will ensure the preservation of principal while
providing the highest investment return with maximum security, meeting the daily cash flow requirements of the
County and conforming to all state statutes governing investment of public funds.

SCOPE

The investment policy applies to all financial assets of the County held by the County Treasurer. These funds are
accounted for in the various funds of the County and include the general fund, special revenue funds, debt service
funds, and capital project funds (unless bond ordinances and resolutions are more restrictive), enterprise funds,
internal service funds, trust and agency funds, and any new fund established by the County.

OBJECTIVE

The primary objectives, in priority order, of the County Treasurer investment activities will be:

      •   Preservation of Principle: Preservation of Principle is the foremost objective in the investment of the County
          funds. Investments will be undertaken in a manner that seeks to ensure the preservation of principal in the
          overall portfolio. To attain this objective, diversification is required so that potential losses on individual
          securities do not exceed the income generated from the remainder of the portfolio.



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                         Page 35 of 55


    •   Credit Risk: The County will minimize credit risk, which is the risk of loss due to the failure of the security
        issuer(s) or backer(s), by:
            o Limiting investments to the types of securities authorized by PA 20 of 1943 (MC: 129.91) as amended,
                 except commercial paper investments must have a rating of not less than P1 from Moody’s or A1 from
                 Standard & Poor’s and mutual fund investments must have a par share intended to maintain a net asset
                 value of at least $1.00 per share. For purposes of this policy, such investments are referred to as
                 securities.
    •   Interest Rate Risk: The County will minimize interest rate risk, which is the risk that the market value of
        securities in the portfolio will fall due to changes in market interest rates, by:
            o Structuring the investment portfolio so that securities mature to meet cash requirements for ongoing
                 operations, thereby avoiding the need to sell securities on the open market prior to maturity.
            o Investing operating funds primarily in shorter-term securities, money market mutual funds, or similar
                 investment pools and limiting the average maturity of the portfolio in accordance with this policy.
    •   Liquidity: The investment portfolio will remain sufficiently liquid to enable the County Treasurer to meet all
        operating requirements which might be reasonably anticipated.
    •   Return Investment: The investment portfolio will be designed with the objective of attaining a rate of return
        throughout budgetary and economic cycles, commensurate with the investment risk constraints and the cash
        flow characteristics of the portfolio. Return on investment is of secondary importance compared to the
        preservation of principle and liquidity objectives described above. The core of investments is limited to
        relatively low risk securities in anticipation of earning a fair return relative to the risk being assumed.
        Securities shall generally be held until maturity with the following exceptions:
            o A security with declining credit may be sold early to minimize loss of principal.
            o A security swap would improve the quality, yield, or target duration in the portfolio.
            o Liquidity needs of the portfolio require that the security be sold.

DIVERSIFICATION

The County Treasurer will diversify the investment portfolio so that the impact of potential losses from any one type of
security or from any one individual will be minimized. With the exception of US Treasury securities, US Agencies, and
authorized investment pools, no more than 25% of the total investment portfolio will be invested in a single security
type.

DELEGATION OF AUTHORITY

Authority to manage the investment program is derived from the following: The Delta County Board of Commissioners
Resolution dated October 25, 1988 and by MCL 48.40. Management responsibility for the investment program is hereby
delegated to the County Treasurer per MCL 48.40, who shall establish written procedures and internal controls for the
operation of the investment program consistent with this investment policy. Procedures should include references to:
Safekeeping, delivery versus payment, investment accounting, purchase agreements, wire transfer agreements,
collateral/depository agreements, and banking service contracts. No person may engage in an investment transaction
except as provided under the terms of this policy and the procedures established by the County Treasurer. The County
Treasurer shall be responsible for all transactions undertaken and shall establish a system of controls to regulate the
activities of subordinate officials.

AUTHORIZED INVESTMENTS

The County Treasurer is authorized to invest in the following types of securities authorized by Public Act 20 of 1943, as
amended:

    •   Bonds, securities, and other obligations of the United States or an agency or instrumentally of the United
        States.




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                      Page 36 of 55


      •   Certificates of deposit, savings accounts, deposit accounts, or depository receipts of a financial institution; but
          only if the financial institution is eligible to be a depository of funds belonging to the State under a law or rule
          of Michigan or the United States.
      •   Commercial paper rated at the time of purchase at the highest classification established by not less than 2
          standard rating services and that matures not more than 270 days after the date of purchase.
      •   Repurchase agreements consisting of instruments in the first bullet point.
      •   Bankers’ acceptances of United States Banks.
      •   Obligations of Michigan or any of its political subdivisions that at the time of purchase are rated as investment
          grade by not less than 1 standard rating service.
      •   Obligations described in bullet points 1 through 6 above, if purchased through an inter-local agreement under
          the urban cooperation act of 1967. (1967 PA 7, MCL 129.141 to 129.150).
      •   Investment pools organized under the surplus funds investment pool act, 1982 PA 367, MCL 129.111 to 129.118.
      •   Investment pools organized under the local government investment pool act, 1985 PA 121, MCL 129.141 to
          129.150.
      •   Mutual funds registered under the investment company act of 1940, maintain a $1.00 per share net asset value
          and with the authority to purchase only investment vehicles that are legal for direct investment by a public
          corporation.

SAFEKEEPING AND CUSTODY

It will be the responsibility of the County Treasurer to determine which securities will be held by a third-party
custodian. Securities held in safekeeping by a third-party custodian will be evidenced by a safekeeping receipt.

PRUDENCE

The standard of prudence to be used by the County Treasurer will be the “prudent person standard” and will be
applied in the context of managing an overall portfolio. Investments will be made with judgment and care, under
circumstances prevailing, which person of prudence, discretion and intelligence exercise in the management of their
own affairs, not for speculation, but for investment, considering the probable safety of their capital as well as the
probable income to be derived.

REPORTING

The County Treasurer will provide an annual report to the Board of Commissioners which provides a clear picture of the
status and types of investments of the current investment portfolio. This report will be prepared in a manner which
will allow the Board of Commissioners to ascertain whether investment activities during the reporting period have
conformed to the investment policy.

9.6       COUNTY ISSUED CREDIT CARDS



9.7       JOURNAL ENTRY/BUDGET AMENDMENTS

PURPOSE

The purpose of this policy is to ensure that all financial adjustments are accurate, authorized, and documented. This
framework prevents unauthorized spending and ensures that the County’s financial statements reflect the true intent
of the Board of Commissioners.

JOURNAL ENTRY (JE) PROCEDURES




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                           Page 37 of 55


Journal entries are used to record transactions or adjustments that do not originate from subsidiary ledgers (like
Payroll or Accounts Payable).

        1.       Preparation: The initiating department must prepare the entry with a clear description of the
                 transaction and the specific General Ledger (GL) lines/accounts affected.
        2.       Segregation of Duties: No single individual shall have the authority to both initiate and post a journal
                 entry. The initiator prepares the entry and attaches the backup documentation applicable to the
                 request. The reviewer/poster, being the County Treasurer, Controller, or Administrator, must verify
                 the entry against the backup documentation before posting to the system.
        3.       Required Documentation: Every JE or budget amendment must include a source document such as an
                 invoice, bank statement, check stub, or calculated spreadsheet, that justifies the dollar amount and
                 the accounts used.

BUDGET AMENDMENT PROCEDURES

A Budget Amendment is required whenever anticipated expenditures exceed the total departmental appropriation
approved by the Board, or when revenue projections shift significantly.

        1.       The Formal Request Process: All requests must be submitted on the County approved Budget
                 Amendment Request Form, as supplied by Administration. This form includes the fund/department
                 name and number, proposed change to line items, and justification for the amendment.
        2.       Funding Source: A budget amendment must be balanced. If an expenditure is increased, the form must
                 identify a corresponding increase in revenue or a decrease in another expenditure line item within the
                 same department.
        3.       Mandatory Backup Documentation: No amendment will be processed without supporting evidence. This
                 includes, but is not limited to: board meeting minutes or resolutions approving the new expenditure,
                 grant award letters or contracts, detailed quotes or estimates for capital purchases, a written narrative
                 explaining the shortfall or the new project/expense.

LEVELS OF APPROVAL

To ensure multi-layered oversight, the following approval hierarchy is established:

        1.       Intra-Departmental (Moving funds between lines within the same department)
                 *Controller
        2.       Inter-Departmental (Moving funds between different departments)
                 *Controller
        3.       Supplemental (Increasing the total budget via Fund Balance
                 *Controller with approval by County Board of Commissioners
ADMINISTRATION AND RECORD KEEPING
        1.       Sequential Numbering: All budget amendments and journal entries must be assigned a unique,
                 sequential number for tracking and audit trails.
        2.       Audit Readiness: All forms and their attached backup documentation must be retained in accordance
                 with State of Michigan Retention Schedules.
        3.       Quarterly Reporting: The Administration Department shall provide the Board of Commissioners with a
                 budget to actual report quarterly.
PROHIBITED ACTIONS
Backdating: Journal Entries and Budget Amendments shall be recorded in the period they occur; backdating to correct
over expenditures is prohibited, unless it’s performed by the Controller during year-end closeout. In which case, refer
to the Fiscal Year-End Closing policy for requirements.




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                        Page 38 of 55


Netting: Revenues and expenses must be recorded gross; they may not be netted against each other to circumvent
budget limitations.

9.8     NSF CHECK



9.9     CAPITAL ASSET

PURPOSE
The purpose of a Capital Asset policy is to provide a consistent framework for identifying and recording long-term
assets. Its specific purposes include: financial reporting, budgetary control, accountability, and standardization. This
policy serves as the backbone for financial management by the County and ensures that significant investments, like
infrastructure, machinery, and facilities, are tracked accurately, maintained properly, and reported in compliance with
Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) requirements.
DEFINITION OF ASSET CLASSES
Assets are typically grouped into classes based on their nature and how they depreciate over time.
        1. Land: Land is unique because it is considered to have inexhaustible life. For this policy definition, it is
           defined as, the surface acreage owned by the County. Included costs are: purchase price, legal fees, land
           clearing, and demolition of existing structures and site preparation. Land is never depreciated.
        2. Land Improvements: These are enhancements made to the land that have a limited life. Examples include:
           fences, parking lots, landscaping, and outdoor lighting. These are depreciated over their specific useful
           lives (often 10 to 20 years).
        3. Buildings and Improvements: This class covers permanent structures and significant renovations that extend
           a building’s life or utility. By definition, this includes: all structures used for operations, such as offices,
           garages, or storage structures. Included costs consist of construction costs, professional fees
           (architect/engineer), and built-in fixtures. Routine maintenance like painting or installation of new
           fixtures, is usually expensed, while a new roof or remodel/renovation would be capitalized as a Building
           Improvement.
        4. Machinery and Equipment: This category includes moveable assets used in daily operations. By definition,
           this pertains to tangible property that is not part of a building or land. Some examples include: heavy
           construction equipment, specialized tools, and office furniture. To be placed in this category the item
           must meet the capitalization threshold and have a useful life of more than one year.
        5. Vehicles: The County maintains fleet vehicles. Each vehicle is licensed and used for County related
           services. Examples include: patrol cars/trucks, parks and recreation cars/trucks, snowplows, dump trucks,
           and transport vehicles. The valuation of these vehicles includes the cost of the chassis plus any specialized
           upfitting required for service.
        6. Infrastructure: These are long-lived capital assets that are normally stationary and can be preserved for a
           significantly greater number of years than more capital assets. Examples include: bridges, roads, trails,
           tunnels, drainage systems, and water/sewer mains. These are often the most complex to track due to their
           scale and the approach to reporting.
ASSET VALUATION - Capital assets of the County are to be valued according to the following criteria:

Donated Assets - For those assets acquired by gift, donation, or payment of a nominal sum which is not reflective of
   the asset's true market value, the cost assigned is to be the fair market value at time of acquisition plus all
   appropriate ancillary costs. If the fair market value is not practicably determinable due to lack of sufficient
   records, estimated cost is to be used.



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                        Page 39 of 55


Purchased Assets - Valuation of purchased assets is to be made on the basis of historical costs including all non-
    refundable purchase taxes (e.g., sales taxes), and all appropriate ancillary costs less any trade discounts or
    rebates. If the historical cost is not practicably determinable, then estimated cost is to be used.
    The cost of extended maintenance/warranty contracts is included in the valuation of the capital asset provided
    the contract is purchased at the same time (or soon thereafter) as the capital asset. These contracts are
    depreciated over the useful life of the asset. Payments for contracts not purchased at the same time as the
    capital asset are not to be capitalized.
Self-Constructed Assets - When the County constructs an asset for its own use, the following policy governs the
    valuation of the asset:

    •   All direct costs associated with the construction are included.
    •   Project management costs may be capitalized directly, when practicably discernible and directly associated
        with the project, or through the application of a percentage of total budgeted project costs. The application
        rate may or may not be designed to recover total agency project management costs. Indirect costs are to be
        excluded unless they are increased by the construction.
    •   Interest costs (if material) incurred during the period of construction should be included in the capitalized
        cost of the asset.
CAPITALIZED ASSETS - Assets capitalized for financial statement purposes.

    All land acquisition, regardless of cost (including ancillary costs), is to be capitalized. All other capital assets
    with a unit cost (including ancillary costs) of $10,000 or greater and an anticipated useful life of more than two
    years are to be capitalized unless otherwise noted.

    For governmental and expendable trust fund type accounts, asset capitalization is recorded in the General Fixed
    Asset Account Group and will be included on the Government-Wide Financial Statements. For proprietary and
    similar trust fund type accounts, the value of the asset is recorded in the account itself. Only capitalized assets
    are considered reportable for financial statement purposes.
New acquisitions - Capital assets which have no relationship to the addition or improvement to, or the repair or
   replacement of a component of, existing fixed assets; and meet the $10,000 and useful life threshold stated
   above are capitalized.
Extraordinary repairs, betterments, or improvements - Outlays that increase future benefits from an existing capital
    asset beyond its previously assessed standard of performance are to be capitalized if they cost $10,000 or more.
    Increased future benefits typically include:

    •   An extension in the estimated useful life of the asset.
    •   An increase in the capacity of an existing capital asset.
    •   A substantial improvement in the quality of output or a reduction in previously assessed operating costs.

Replacement - Acquisition of a capital asset to replace part of another fixed asset is capitalized when the cost of the
   replacement is $10,000 or more AND equal to at least the lesser of 10% of replacement value of the asset or
   $100,000.
    Exceptions to this policy are:

    •   Replacement roof coverings are not capitalized (whether or not the replacement is with superior materials)
        unless the replacement extends the useful life of the entire building.
    •   Replacement floor coverings and window coverings are not capitalized.
    •   Costs to remodel or convert a building to a different use, where the remodeling does not include structural
        changes or extend the useful life of the structure itself are not capitalized.
    The cost and accumulated depreciation of the replaced capital asset is to be removed from the accounting
    records if the amounts are determinable and the replacement is capitalized. For proprietary and similar trust
    fund type accounts, the removed amounts are offset by entries to Revenue "Gain or Loss on Sale of Fixed Assets”.



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                        Page 40 of 55


Addition - A $10,000 or more expansion of or extension to an existing capitalized fixed asset is capitalized.
Bulk Purchase - Bulk purchases of like capital assets with unit costs of less than $10,000 may be capitalized as a group
    where the assets are interdependent and are not likely to be disposed of individually. Example: IT equipment
    (laptops), copy machines.
SERVERS AND NETWORK EQUIPMENT

    A network is determined to consist of individual components that may be below the $10,000 threshold, but are
    interdependent and the overriding value to the County is on the entire network and not the individual assets.
    The components that are a part of the computer network and have a useful life less than 5 years would not be
    capitalized unless the individual assets and related equipment have a cost greater than $10,000.
LEASED ASSETS AND SUBSCRIPTION BASED INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY ARRANGEMENTS

    When the County is the lessee for non-cancellable leases of buildings or equipment with an initial individual value
    over $10,000, the County will recognize an intangible right-to-use lease asset that is capitalized over the term
    of the lease. The lease asset is initially measured as the initial amount of the lease liability, adjusted for lease
    payments made at or before the lease commencement date, plus certain initial direct costs. Subsequently, the
    lease asset is amortized on a straight-line basis over its useful life.
    When the County has a non-cancellable subscription-based information technology arrangement (SBITA) with an
    initial individual value over $10,000, the County will recognize an intangible right-to-use subscription asset that
    is capitalized over the term of the agreement. The subscription asset is initially measured as the initial amount
    of the subscription liability, adjusted for payments made at or before the agreement commencement date, plus
    certain initial direct costs. Subsequently, the subscription asset is amortized on a straight-line basis over its
    useful life.
CONSTRUCTION IN PROGRESS

    Assets that are constructed by or for the County are included as construction in progress in the asset system until
    they are placed into service. Once placed into service, the asset is placed in the appropriate asset class and
    capitalized. The amount to be capitalized includes all costs of the project including any interest on debt relating
    to the project while under construction. Any residual amount spent after the initial capitalization will be added
    to the base asset.
DEPRECIATION POLICY

    Depreciation is to be calculated and recorded for all capitalized capital assets with the exception of land and
    construction in progress.
    Depreciation is to be calculated, using the straight-line method:
    Annual Depreciation =              Cost
                                 Asset Useful Life
        Asset Class              Typical Useful Life              Depreciable?
        Land                     N/A                              No
        Buildings                30-50 Years                      Yes
        Vehicles                 5-10 Years                       Yes
        Equipment                5-15 Years                       Yes
        Infrastructure           20-50 Years                      Yes
        Technology               3-10 Years                       Yes

USEFUL LIFE




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                          Page 41 of 55


    The useful life of an asset is the estimated number of years an asset will remain in service while generating
    financial value. When possible, the useful life of an asset should be determined based on actual past experiences
    with similar assets owned by the County. In situations where the documentation of the County’s actual past
    experiences with similar assets is not adequate for determining the useful life of an asset, the useful life may be
    determined by utilizing external sources such as generally accepted industry standards and recommendations,
    expert opinions, referring to IRS Publication 946, Appendix B, or useful life schedules published by other
    governmental agencies.
INVENTORY POLICY

    The following capital assets are to be considered inventoriable assets and carried on the property records of
    the county:

    •   Land.
    •   Capital assets with a unit cost (including sales tax and ancillary costs) of $10,000 or greater, unless
        otherwise noted.


    Departments are responsible to take inventory annually and report any unrecorded disposals or acquisitions to
    the Department of Administration.
INFRASTRUCTURE

    Infrastructure includes public domain general capital assets that are immovable and of value only to the County.
    Such assets normally do not impact the value of the land on which they are located. Infrastructure includes
    certain improvements other than buildings such as roads, bridges, curbs, gutters, streets, sidewalks, drainage
    systems, lighting systems, and similar assets. All land and improvements within the operating right-of-way of the
    County’s transportation system are considered infrastructure.

    When streets, sidewalks, lighting systems, and other similar capital assets are located in a confined setting such
    as a campus, park, or compound and they meet the County’s capitalization policy, they are capitalized and
    inventoried under the classification of land improvements or infrastructure.
    Infrastructure with a historical cost (or value if donated) of $50,000 or more is to be capitalized for financial
    statement purposes.
RECORDS REQUIREMENTS

    The County is to maintain a capital asset system which includes records for all inventoriable assets - assets with
    a cost of $10,000 or more.
REMOVAL OF ASSETS FROM INVENTORY

    Capital assets are removed from active inventory once the asset is disposed of and no longer owned by the
    County.
    Disposed capital assets remain part of the County’s fixed asset inventory as an inactive capital asset until the
    fiscal year in which the asset was disposed is audited.

9.10    FEDERAL AWARDS



9.11    CASH HANDLING

PURPOSE AND SCOPE




As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                        Page 42 of 55


Cash and cash equivalents apply to currency, coin, checks, credit and debit card payments, other electronic payment
media, and negotiable instruments payable to the County. This Policy governs the handling of County cash, as directed
by:
    • MCL 48.40 - empowering the County Treasurer to be responsible for County funds; and
    • MCL 46.11 - empowering the County Board of Commissioners to establish rules and regulations in reference to
       the management of the interest and business of the County; and
    • MCL 750.490 - governing all public moneys which come into the possession of any Elected Official, Appointed
       Official, Department Head, or employee with steps to ensure its safekeeping.
AUTHORITY IN COUNTY TREASURER
The County Treasurer is authorized to establish procedures for the receipt, handling, and deposits of County funds by
any Elected Official, Appointed Official, Department Head, or employee. The procedures are subject to approval by the
County Administrator and the County Board of Commissioners. Approval by the Treasurer’s Office is required for any
changes in the cash handling policy and procedures.

The County Treasurer:
   • will conduct periodic reviews/audits of cash handling procedures; and
   • has the authority to approve or rescind authorization of cash handlers; and
   • will inspect departmental cash records including overages and shortages; and
   • will inspect departmental practices and procedures; and
   • may require that the cash handler complete a course of instruction on the secure processing of money, consistent
       with this policy; and
   • must authorize any Department or Office to receive cash (cash collection point), unless otherwise permitted by
       State Statute. A Department or Office requesting to establish a cash collection point must submit a request to
       the County Treasurer providing the following information:
           o reason for the cash collection point; and
           o listing of positions/employees involved with the cash collection point, a description of their duties, and
               how segregation of duties will be maintained; and
           o will there be a need for any cash drawers; and
           o a description of the reconciliation process; and
           o a description of the process for safeguarding cash until it is deposited; and
           o a description of the frequency of deposits.
The request will be reviewed by the County Treasurer and submitted to the County Administrator and County Board with
a recommendation to approve or deny the request.
SUMMARY
Cash handling is an important role and carries great responsibility. The Treasurer’s Office has established this cash
handling policy and procedures to provide direction for county departments and employees. Cash handler training is
available through the Treasurer’s office.
INTERNAL CONTROLS
Strong internal controls for cash collection are necessary to prevent mishandling of funds, and are designed to safeguard
and protect employees from inappropriate charges of mishandling funds by defining their responsibilities in the cash
handling process. All incoming County cash and cash equivalents must:
    • be accounted for as received and a receipt made; and
    • be received in view of the customer until transaction is complete; and
    • be removed from the workstation after each transaction and before the cash handler leaves the workstation; and
    • never be left unattended or accessible to unauthorized persons; and
    • be in a secured cash box, register, or safe and locked when not in use; and
    • be secured in a safe if held overnight; and
    • be kept to a minimum at all times; excess funds should be stored in a safe or deposited with the Treasurer’s
        Office; and
    • never be used for making change outside of the transaction the cash is received, petty cash purposes,
        expenditures, refunds, etc.; and
    • when reconciling and balancing, cash must be counted out of sight of the general public; and
    • cash over/short variations must be investigated and resolved to the extent possible; and



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                      Page 43 of 55


    •   any cash over/short variations must be reported to the County Treasurer; and
    •   monthly reconciliation of daily deposit tickets to bank statements is to be completed by the Treasurer’s Office.
CUSTODY
Cash Handling and Responsibilities for Elected Officials, Appointed Officials, & Department Heads:
   • designate and train employees on proper cash handling practices consistent with this policy; and
   • provide safekeeping, and timely and accurate deposit of cash and cash equivalents to the Treasurer’s Office; and
   • ensure adequate separation of duties including cash collecting, depositing, reconciling, and reporting; and
   • notify the County Treasurer and County Administrator of any loss or theft of County cash immediately upon
       discovery, or any continuing pattern of loss in excess of twenty dollars ($20.00); and within 24 hours have a
       written notice of discovery to both entities; and
   • allow for onsite inspections and observations of cash handling processing, procedures, and reports by the County
       Treasurer; and
   • protect employees from risk by following established policies and procedures.
Cash Handling and Responsibilities for Cash Handlers:
   • be authorized for cash handling by the Elected Official, Appointed Official, or Department Head; and
   • be responsible for the integrity of cash and cash equivalents in their possession; and
   • keep cash and cash equivalents in a consistent manner and to a minimum at all times; and
   • balance and deposit cash and cash equivalents daily; and
   • report any over/shortage immediately to their supervisor; and
   • notify supervisor of any loss or theft of County cash immediately upon discovery; and
   • under no circumstance should the cash handler commingle cash and cash equivalents with their own personal
       funds; and
   • comply with all cash handling policies and procedures.
REPORTING
All cash receipts and related documents must be maintained in accordance with the State of Michigan Record
Retention schedules. Cash drawer reconciliation sheets, computerized reports, bank deposit slips, credit card receipts,
manual cash receipts, etc. must be retained for the current year + seven (7) years, by the Treasurer’s Office.
PROCEDURES
Preparation of Deposits:
    1. Deliver deposits to Treasurer’s Office by 2 PM EST; deposits after cutoff may be processed the next business day.
    2. Checks must be payable to Delta County and endorsed accordingly.
    3. Third-party checks are not accepted - the bank will not accept them for deposit.
    4. Departments will utilize deposit tickets obtained from the Treasurer’s Office for the applicable account.
    5. Bundle and total checks - attach calculator tape (if applicable).
    6. Count cash - attach calculator tape, if applicable.
    7. Record cash, coin, and total of checks on deposit ticket and calculator tape must be included with deposit.
    8. Attach backup documentation for credit/debit card transactions. Do not list on deposit ticket.
Cash Over/Short:
   1. Recount coin and currency.
   2. Recount cash equivalents.
   3. Verify figures on applicable accounting system.
   4. Verify each transaction.
   5. Search cash handling area or physical garbage basket for any cash or cash equivalents. Retain contents of garbage
       basket, if necessary, until deposit is reconciled.
   6. If unable to locate the discrepancy, report the cash over/short to Elected Official, Appointed Official, or
       Department Head and to the Treasurer’s Office immediately.
   7. The Elected Official, Appointed Official, Department Head or Treasurer’s Office shall follow the steps above to
       reconcile the deposit. If the discrepancy cannot be resolved, it must be documented with that day’s activity and
       reported to the County Administrator.
Petty Cash:
    1. Petty Cash is cash approved by the County Board of Commissioners on a case-by-case basis for use to make
        incidental purchases where there is an immediate need and the purchase cannot be charged at the point of
        service.



As Amended: 06/02/2026                                                                                     Page 44 of 55


    2. Departments may need petty cash to make incidental purchases. While this practice is discouraged, the County
       Board realizes there are extenuating circumstances that necessitates the use of petty cash funds.
    3. The total of the receipts in the drawer, plus the cash in the drawer, must always equal the total authorized petty
       cash fund.
    4. To obtain or increase a petty cash fund, the department must submit a request to the County Administrator
       stating the need for the request.
    5. The expenditures cannot exceed the amount approved for the petty cash fund. The expenditures cannot cause
       the budgeted line items to be exceeded.
    6. The expenditures made using a petty cash fund are subject to the provisions of the Purchasing Policy.
    7. To replenish a petty cash fund, the department must submit a voucher to the County Clerk’s Office. The Clerk
       will produce a check payable to the County Treasurer-Petty Cash. The Treasurer will cash the check. The
       requesting department will verify cash and initial check stub upon receipt of the cash.
    8. Refer to 9.2     Purchasing and Procurement for established petty cash fund and limits.
Start-Up Cash:
    1. Departments who take payments from the public need start-up cash to establish cash drawers.
    2. Start up cash is a cash drawer approved by the Board of Commissioners on a case-by-case basis for use as start-
        up funds in cash drawers for making change for customers. No expenses should come out of this cash and the
        amount approved should always be the amount in the drawer after balancing at the end of each day.
    3. To obtain an increase to start-up cash, the department must notify the County Administrator stating the need
        for the request.
    4. The disbursement issuing the cash is set up as an asset in the General Ledger.
    5. Refer to 9.11 Cash Handling For Cashiers portion of this policy for use of these funds.
